Holographic Characterisation of Locally Anti-de Sitter Spacetimes
Alex McGill

Abstract
. It is shown that an ( n + 1 ) (n+1) –dimensional asymptotically anti-de Sitter solution of the Einstein-vacuum equations is locally isometric to pure anti-de Sitter spacetime near the conformal boundary if and only if the boundary metric is conformally flat and (for n ≠ 4 n\neq 4 ) the boundary stress-energy tensor vanishes, subject to (i) sufficient (finite) regularity in the metric and (ii) the satisfaction of a geometric criterion on the boundary. A key tool in the proof is the Carleman estimate of [ MS20 ] , which is applied to prove a unique continuation result for the Weyl curvature at the conformal boundary given vanishing to sufficiently high order over a sufficiently long timespan.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

Asymptotically anti-de Sitter (aAdS) spacetimes play a central role in the conjectured
AdS/CFT correspondence

[
Mal99
]
, which posits a duality between the bulk gravitational theory in such spacetimes and
conformal field theories
(CFT) living on their lower–dimensional ‘boundaries’. In this sense, it is a realisation of the more general
holographic principle
.

In order to rigorously determine the validity of this conjecture, one should aim to prove a
1-1
correspondence between
(n+1)
–dimensional aAdS solutions of the Einstein-vacuum equations with a (conveniently normalised) negative cosmological constant
\Lambda
,
1
1

1

Here
Rc
and
Rs
represent the
g
–Ricci and scalar curvatures respectively.

(1.1)

Rc-\frac{1}{2}Rs\cdot g+\Lambda\cdot g=0\text{,}\qquad\Lambda:=-\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\text{.}

and suitably-defined ‘data’ which characterises a particular boundary CFT. To this end, significant progress has been made in the
Riemannian

[
Biq08
]
and
stationary Lorentzian

[
CD11
]
cases. The series of articles
[
HS16
,
HS17
,
MS20
]
initiated the study of the
non-stationary Lorentzian
setting by considering uniqueness properties of wave equations on fixed aAdS backgrounds near the boundary. The upcoming work
[
HSon
]
aims to use the theoretical framework developed in these articles to address the ‘full’ problem (i.e. considering solutions of (
1.1
) rather than wave equations on fixed backgrounds). The present article aims to demonstrate that, even without applying the thus-far developed theory to the Einstein-vacuum equations themselves, this framework is already sufficiently powerful to prove physically interesting correspondence statements.

Specifically, we will provide necessary and sufficient conditions on the boundary data for a given aAdS solution of (
1.1
) to be locally isometric to the
pure AdS
spacetime
2
2

2

This is the maximally symmetric solution of (
1.1
).
near the boundary. Such spacetimes locally have constant curvature but may have nontrivial global topology- see, for example, the so-called
BTZ black holes
in (2+1) dimensions
[
BTZ92
,
Bañ+93
]
and their higher-dimensional counterparts
[
Åmi+96
,
Bañ98
]
, which arise via identification of points along orbits of Killing vectors in pure AdS.
3
3

3

As the name suggests, these
topological black holes
have the global structure of a black hole; they may be characterised by a ‘mass’ and and ‘angular momentum’, they have horizons and it has been shown that they form via gravitational collapse
[
MR93
]
.

The article
[
SS00
]
identified the boundary data defined by such spacetimes; the contribution of the present article is to prove the converse statement. We therefore have a rigidity statement for vacuum aAdS spacetimes locally isometric to pure AdS near the boundary that is formulated entirely in terms of boundary data. In other words, it is holographic in nature.

The preceding discussion raises the following questions which will be informally addressed in the remainder of this section:

•

In what sense does an aAdS spacetime have a boundary?

•

What constitutes appropriate boundary data in this setting?

•

What is the precise statement of the holographic rigidity result?

•

How does this result follow from the framework developed in the above-referenced series of articles?

1.2.
aAdS Spacetimes and the Correspondence Problem

Pure AdS spacetime is the maximally symmetric solution of (
1.1
). It has the representation
(\mathscr{M}_{0},g_{0})
, where

(1.2)

\mathscr{M}_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\text{,}\qquad g_{0}=(1+r^{2})^{-1}dr^{2}-(1+r^{2})dt^{2}+r^{2}\cdot\mathring{\gamma}\text{.}

Here, we’ve covered the manifold using polar coordinates;
\mathring{\gamma}
is the unit round metric. Under the coordinate transformation
r=\frac{1}{4}\rho^{-1}(2+\rho)(2-\rho)
it has the representation
(\mathscr{M},g)
, where

(1.3)

\mathscr{M}=(0,2]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\text{,}\qquad g=\rho^{-2}\left[d\rho^{2}-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}\left(dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}\right)+\frac{1}{16}\rho^{4}\left(-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma}\right)\right]\text{.}

Here,
\rho
is the coordinate for the
(0,2]
component. In particular, we may attach a timelike
conformal boundary

(1.4)

(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})\simeq(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1},-dt^{2}+\mathring{\gamma})

to
\rho^{2}g
at
\rho=0
.

An
asymptotically
anti-de Sitter (aAdS) spacetime region
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
has a manifold of the form

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}

\displaystyle=(0,\rho_{0}]\times\mathscr{I}\text{,}\qquad\rho_{0}>0\text{,}

for smooth manifolds
\mathscr{I}
. On such
\mathscr{M}
, it is natural to consider so-called
vertical
tensor fields, which only have components in directions tangential to
\mathscr{I}
. In other words, a vertical tensor field on an aAdS region
\mathscr{M}
can be viewed as a
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
–parametrised family of tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
. They thus admit an intuitive and consistent notion of ‘boundary limit’ as
\rho\searrow 0
.

On these manifolds, we consider metrics of the form

(1.6)

\displaystyle g=\rho^{-2}\left(d\rho^{2}+\mathsf{g}_{ab}\,dx^{a}dx^{b}\right)\text{,}

where
\mathsf{g}
is a vertical tensor field (
x^{a}
are
\rho
–transported coordinates on
\mathscr{I}
) and there exists some Lorentzian metric
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathscr{I}
such that
\mathsf{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
as
\rho\searrow 0
.
\left(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}\right)
is then the conformal boundary associated with
\left(\mathscr{M},g,\rho\right)
and the form (
1.6
) is referred to as
Fefferman-Graham gauge

[
FG85
]
. Accordingly, we refer to this class of spacetimes as
FG-aAdS segments
.

Remark 1.1
.

Imposing Fefferman-Graham gauge does not result in any loss of generality. One can always transform more general spacetime metrics with the same
\rho\searrow 0
asymptotics into the form (
1.6
) via an appropriate change of coordinates. See, for example,
[
GL91
,
GW99
,
Gra99
]
.

In this article, we will restrict our attention to
vacuum solutions
, i.e. to those FG-aAdS segments whose metrics solve (
1.1
). In
[
Sha20
]
, it was demonstrated that such segments admit partial expansions at the conformal boundary
\mathscr{I}
even for vertical metrics of only finite regularity:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\rho^{2k}\mathfrak{g}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n}\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}+\rho^{n}\mathsf{r}\qquad&\text{$n$ odd,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\rho^{2k}\mathfrak{g}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n}\log\rho\,\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}+\rho^{n}\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}+\rho^{n}\mathsf{r}\qquad&\text{$n$ even,}\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{r}
is a vertical tensor field with vanishing boundary limit and
\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}=\mathfrak{g}
is the conformal boundary metric introduced above. Of particular interest is the fact that all terms below order
n
and the trace/divergence of
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
are formally determined by
\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}
(itself is
not
formally determined by (
1.1
)), while all terms beyond order
n
are formally determined by
\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}
and the trace/divergence–free part of
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
(also not formally determined by (
1.1
))
4
4

4

In the context of the AdS/CFT correspondence,
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
arises from the expectation value of the boundary CFT stress-energy tensor
[
DSS01
]
.
. This identifies the pair
\left(\mathfrak{g}^{(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}\right)
as appropriate data that one could prescribe on
\mathscr{I}
in an attempt to characterise the bulk spacetime.

An important observation is that there exist coordinate transformations
\left(\rho,x^{a}\right)\to\left(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{x}^{a}\right)
preserving the Fefferman-Graham gauge (
1.6
), i.e.

(1.8)

\displaystyle g=\tilde{\rho}^{-2}\left(d\tilde{\rho}^{2}+\tilde{\mathsf{g}}_{ab}\,d\tilde{x}^{a}d\tilde{x}^{b}\right)\text{,}

but altering the coefficients in the partial expansion of the corresponding vertical metric
\tilde{\mathsf{g}}
(now given in terms of
\tilde{\rho}
). In particular, such a coordinate transformation induces a
conformal transformation
of
\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}
; for the higher-order coefficients, the effect is more complicated
[
Imb+00
,
Ske01
]
. Since the underlying physical theory is invariant under such transformations, it therefore only makes sense for us to speak of
gauge-equivalent
classes of boundary data
\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}\right]
in the above sense.

A naive way to rigorously formulate the above-described AdS/CFT problem would be as a
boundary Cauchy problem
for (
1.1
) with data
\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}\right]
prescribed on
\mathscr{I}
. However, in analogy with the boundary Cauchy problem for the wave equation on a cylinder, one in fact expects this problem to be
ill-posed
given general (i.e. possibly non-analytic) boundary data. Nevertheless, it remains apposite to ask whether solutions, if they exist, remain unique:

Question 1.2
.

Assuming that a solution of (
1.1
) exists, is it uniquely determined in the bulk (up to isometry) by
\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}\right]
?

1.3.
The Rigidity Result

Let’s consider the above question in the case of pure AdS. In
[
SS00
]
, the authors proved that if a vacuum FG-aAdS segment (
n>2
) is locally isometric to pure AdS
5
5

5

Not
necessarily globally isometric to pure AdS; the boundary topology could differ from that given in (
1.4
).
then its metric is given near the conformal boundary by

(1.9)

\displaystyle g=\rho^{-2}\left(d\rho^{2}+\mathsf{g}_{ab}\right)\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{ab}-\mathfrak{p}_{ab}\cdot\rho^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\,\mathfrak{g}^{cd}\,\mathfrak{p}_{ac}\,\mathfrak{p}_{bd}\cdot\rho^{4}\text{,}

where
\mathfrak{g}
is conformally flat and
\mathfrak{p}
is the
\mathfrak{g}
–
Schouten tensor

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}:=\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\mathfrak{Rc}-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\mathfrak{Rs}\cdot\mathfrak{g}\right)\text{,}

in which
\mathfrak{Rc}
and
\mathfrak{Rs}
are the
\mathfrak{g}
–Ricci and scalar curvatures respectively.

It is then natural to ask if the converse statement is true, i.e. if a vacuum FG-aAdS segment
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
has boundary data
\left[\mathfrak{g}^{(0)},\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}\right]
in which
\mathfrak{g}
is conformally flat and

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\,\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\,\mathfrak{p}\,\mathfrak{p}\qquad&\text{,}\qquad n=4\text{,}\\
0\qquad&\text{,}\qquad n\neq 4\text{,}\end{cases}

must
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
be locally isometric to pure AdS near
\mathscr{I}
? In this article we provide a positive answer to this question subject to a key geometric condition on the boundary that was first introduced in
[
MS20
]
:

Definition 1.3
.
6
6

6

This definition is stated in terms of a smooth time function
t
on the boundary. Formally, this must be a restriction of a
global time
as given in Definition
5.1
.

Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a vacuum FG-aAdS segment. We say that the
null convexity criterion
is satisfied on
\left(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g}\right)
if the bounds

(1.12)

\mathfrak{p}(Z,Z)\geq C(Zt)^{2}\text{,}\qquad|\mathfrak{D}^{2}_{ZZ}t|\leq B(Zt)^{2}\text{,}

hold for some constants
0\leq B<C
, where
Z
is any vector field on
\mathscr{I}
satisfying
\mathfrak{g}(Z,Z)=0
and
\mathfrak{D}
is the
\mathfrak{g}
–Levi-Civita connection.

The main result of this article may now be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.4
.
7
7

7

The rigorous statement of this result in Theorem
6.6
makes precise sense of ‘near’ using Definition
5.4
.

Fix
n>2
. Suppose
(\mathscr{M},g)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional vacuum FG-aAdS segment for which:

•

\mathsf{g}
is sufficiently (finitely) regular.

•

\mathscr{I}
has compact cross-sections.

•

The null convexity criterion (
1.12
) is satisfied.

•

\mathscr{T}=\mathscr{I}\cap\{t_{0}<t<t_{1}\}
is a sufficiently long timespan on the boundary.
8
8

8

Determined by the constants
B
and
C
featuring in the null convexity criterion.

Then
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is locally isometric to pure AdS near
\mathscr{T}
if and only if
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
has boundary data for which the following hold on
\mathscr{T}
:

•

\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}
is conformally flat.

•

\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\,\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\cdot\mathfrak{p}\cdot\mathfrak{p}\text{,}\qquad&n=4\text{,}\\
0\text{,}\qquad&n\neq 4\text{.}\end{cases}

Remark 1.5
.

Proposition
6.7
demonstrates that the stated conditions on
\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
in Theorem
7
are ‘gauge-invariant’ in the sense that if one representative of a gauge-equivalent class of boundary data satisfies these conditions (and therefore corresponds to a FG-aAdS segment that is locally isometric to pure AdS) then the same must be true for all other representatives of the class.

Remark 1.6
.

The null convexity criterion as given in (
1.12
) is gauge-dependent in the sense that it may hold for one representative of a gauge-equivalent class (in the above-defined sense) but not for another. However, for our purposes it is sufficient to demonstrate that (
1.12
) holds for one particular representative of the gauge-equivalent class; once this is obtained, its key role is in the construction of a
pseudoconvex foliation

[
HS16
,
HS17
,
MS20
]
of the near-boundary region (a gauge-invariant notion).

In the upcoming work
[
CSon
]
the authors formulate a gauge-invariant version of the null convexity criterion. One may straightforwardly replace the null convexity assumption and associated constructions in Theorem
7
with this generalised criterion.

1.4.
Background and Proof Outline

1.4.1.
Unique Continuation for the Weyl Curvature

The
Weyl curvature
is the traceless part of the Riemann tensor; if it vanishes for a solution of (
1.1
) then the solution has constant curvature (i.e. it is
maximally symmetric
) and is thus locally isometric to pure AdS since (
1.1
) involves a negative cosmological constant
[
Car04
]
. The key observation is that the Weyl curvature of a vacuum FG-aAdS segment satisfies a wave equation. Hence, to prove our result, we expect to be able to apply the theory developed in the sequence of articles
[
HS16
,
HS17
,
MS20
]
which considered tensorial solutions to wave equations of the form

(1.13)

(\Box_{g}+\sigma)u=\mathcal{G}(u,\nabla u)\text{,}\qquad\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\text{,}

on fixed aAdS backgrounds
9
9

9

\mathcal{G}(u,\nabla u)
represents lower-order, possibly non-linear terms.
. Specifically, these articles aimed to determine if a solution
u
of (
1.13
) with vanishing boundary data on
\mathscr{I}
must necessarily vanish in the interior. This is what is known as a
unique continuation
problem.

A positive answer to this problem was provided in the first of these articles,
[
HS16
]
. However, this rested on the assumptions that:

(1)

The boundary metric is static.

(2)

u
vanishes at a sufficiently fast rate
along a sufficiently long timespan
on
\mathscr{I}
.

The first assumption constitutes a significant restriction on the class of spacetimes for which the result may be applied. Furthermore, it was not known if the second assumption was strictly necessary.

The subsequent article
[
HS17
]
weakened assumption (1) by generalising to spacetimes with boundary metrics of only bounded ‘non-stationarity’. However, this still left open the question of whether assumption (2) could be weakened (or even removed).

Most recently,
[
MS20
]
further weakened assumption (1) by permitting spacetimes with general time functions; the new assumption was then formulated as the null convexity criterion (
1.12
). This article also rigorously justified the necessity of assumption (2) by linking it to the trajectories of

21 warnings
near-boundary null geodesics. Specifically, upper and lower bounds (depending on the constants
B,C
featuring in (
1.12
)) were proved on the ‘time of return’ of such geodesics to
\mathscr{I}
. Since counterexamples to unique continuation were constructed in
[
AB95
]
using geometric optics methods with these geodesics,
10
10

10

These counterexamples were only constructed for the case when the mass
\sigma
is the specific value that makes the corresponding Klein-Gordon equation conformally invariant. In the upcoming work
[
Guion
]
the authors intend to extend the construction to the non-conformal case.
the bounds show that there is:

•

A maximum timespan across which such counterexamples may be constructed.

•

A minimum timespan across which we must assume vanishing of our field in order to eliminate the possibility of such counterexamples existing. Crucially, this minimum timespan matches the one in assumption (2).

FG-aAdS segments are constructed in such a way that it is natural to view tensor fields as
mixed
, containing vertical components that are treated using
\mathsf{g}
and spacetime components that are treated using
g
.
[
MS20
]
accordingly developed a mixed covariant formalism which makes sense of higher-order derivatives acting on vertical tensor fields. To this end, an extension
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
of the
\mathsf{g}
–Levi-Civita connection
\mathsf{D}
is constructed
11
11

11

In exactly such a way that it acts as a tensor derivation and is
\mathsf{g}
-compatible.
so as to permit covariant derivatives of vertical tensor fields in all
\mathscr{M}
–directions. A ‘mixed’ connection
\bar{\nabla}
is then defined in such a way that it acts as the standard
g
–Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
on the spacetime components and
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
on the vertical components of a given tensor field; the vertical wave operator
\bar{\Box}_{g}
is defined as the
g
–trace of
\bar{\nabla}^{2}
.

Working in this framework, the article studied vertical tensor field solutions
\mathsf{u}
of wave equations of the form

(1.14)

\displaystyle(\bar{\Box}_{g}+\sigma)\mathsf{u}=\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{u},\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{u})\text{,}\qquad\sigma\in\mathbb{R}\text{.}

The main result was as follows:

Theorem 1.7
.

[
MS20
]

Assume the following:

•

(\mathscr{M},g)
is a FG-aAdS segment satisfying the null convexity criterion (
1.12
).

•

There is some
p>0
such that
\mathcal{G}
in (
1.14
) satisfies the bound

(1.15)

|\mathcal{G}(\mathsf{u},\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{u})|^{2}\lesssim\rho^{4+p}|\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{u}|^{2}+\rho^{3p}|\mathsf{u}|^{2}\text{.}

•

\mathsf{u}
is a solution of (
1.14
) for which—for sufficiently large
\kappa
depending on
\sigma
,
\mathsf{g}
,
t
and the rank of
\mathsf{u}
—the limit

(1.16)

\rho^{-\kappa}\mathsf{u}\to 0\text{,}\qquad\rho\searrow 0\text{,}

holds over a sufficiently long timespan
\mathscr{T}\subseteq\mathscr{I}
determined by
B,C
from (
1.12
).

Then
\mathsf{u}\equiv 0
in some interior neighbourhood of
\mathscr{T}
.
12
12

12

In general, one also requires a compact support assumption for
\mathsf{u}
on level sets of
(\rho,t)
. In our case this need not be of concern since we assume
\mathscr{I}
has compact cross-sections.

The key tool in the proof of this unique continuation statement was a novel
Carleman estimate
, a form of which we state in Theorem
5.5
before applying for our own purposes in the present article.

The spacetime wave equation for the Weyl curvature can be decomposed into a system of vertical wave equations for the vertical tensor fields

(1.17)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}:=\rho^{2}W_{abcd}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{1}_{abc}:=\rho^{2}W_{\rho abc}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}:=\rho^{2}W_{\rho a\rho b}\text{,}

that, together, fully determine the spacetime Weyl curvature. Crucially, the lower-order nonlinearities in these equations satisfy (
1.15
). Theorem
1.7
is used to demonstrated that the spacetime Weyl curvature identically vanishes near the boundary given vanishing to sufficiently high order on approach to the boundary for
\mathsf{W}^{0}
,
\mathsf{W}^{1}
and
\mathsf{W}^{2}
.
13
13

13

These ‘vanishing rates’ should be understood in the context of the Fefferman-Graham expansions for the vertical components of the Weyl curvature as given in Corollary
4.11
.

1.4.2.
Connection to the Boundary Data

[
Sha20
, Proposition 2.25]
uses (
1.1
) to relate each of the vertical components of the Weyl curvature to vertical metric quantities:

(1.18)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{abcd}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{a[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]b}+\rho^{-1}\left(\mathsf{g}_{a[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]b}-\mathsf{g}_{b[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]a}\right)\text{,}

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{1}_{cab}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{[b}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{a]c}\text{,}

(1.20)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{2}\mathsf{g}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ab}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ac}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{bd}\text{.}

Moreover, we have boundary expansions for the right-hand sides in which the leading-order terms feature the
\mathfrak{g}
–Weyl and Cotton tensors
\mathfrak{W}
and
\mathfrak{C}
, whose vanishing follows from the condition that
\mathfrak{g}
is conformally flat:
14
14

14

See Proposition
4.12
for a precise statements and proof of these leading order expressions.

(1.21)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}=\mathfrak{W}+o\left(1\right)\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{1}=\frac{\rho}{2(n-2)}\mathfrak{C}+o\left(\rho\right)\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{2}=o\left(1\right)\text{.}

This yields a base level of vanishing for each of
\mathsf{W}^{0}
,
\mathsf{W}^{1}
and
\mathsf{W}^{2}
which we improve by making use of the equations obtained by expressing the second Bianchi identity in terms of vertical objects (see Proposition (
4.13
)). Iteratively substituting the partial Fefferman-Graham expansions of
\mathsf{W}^{0}
,
\mathsf{W}^{1}
and
\mathsf{W}^{2}
into these equations and applying the condition on
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
, we obtain

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}=o(\rho^{n-2})\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{1}=o(\rho^{n-1})\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{2}=o(\rho^{n-2})\text{.}

Once this is obtained, it is possible to iteratively integrate the vertical Bianchi equations; at each iteration, degrees of vertical regularity are exchanged for additional orders of vanishing. The process is continued until the vanishing rate required for unique continuation is obtained.

1.5.
Organisation

•

In Section
2
we formally define FG-aAdS segments. Vertical tensor fields are introduced and a notion of boundary limits for such objects is established.

•

In Section
3
we introduce the mixed tensor calculus which will enable us to make sense of a wave operator acting on a vertical tensor field in a consistent way. We also present formulae used to convert spacetime equations into their mixed counterparts.

•

In Section
4
, we study FG-aAdS segments whose metrics solve (
1.1
). The spacetime Weyl curvature for such segments satisfies a wave equation; we use the tools developed in Section
3
to decompose this into a system of wave equations for each of the vertical components of the spacetime Weyl curvature.

•

In Section
5
we introduce global time functions and state the Carleman estimate from
[
MS20
]
in a form suited to our purposes.

•

In Section
6
we prove some preliminary results before stating and proving the main result of this article.

3.7
and
3.9
.

2.
Asymptotically AdS Spacetimes

We begin by recalling some basic definitions from
[
MS20
]
concerning the spacetime manifolds on which we will be working and the natural tensor fields to consider on them.

Definition 2.1
.

An
aAdS region
is a manifold of the form

(2.1)

\mathscr{M}:=(0,\rho_{0}]\times\mathscr{I}\text{,}\qquad\rho_{0}>0\text{,}

in which
\mathscr{I}
is a smooth
n
-dimensional manifold for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Given an aAdS region
\mathscr{M}
,
\rho
denotes the coordinate function on
\mathscr{M}
projecting onto the
(0,\rho_{0}]
-component and
\partial_{\rho}
denotes the
\mathscr{M}
–lift of the canonical vector field
d\rho
on
(0,\rho_{0}]
.

Definition 2.2
.

The
vertical bundle

\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
of rank
(k,l)
over
\mathscr{M}
is the manifold of all rank
(k,l)
tensors on level sets of
\rho
in
\mathscr{M}
:

(2.2)

\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}=\bigcup_{\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]}T^{k}_{l}\{\rho=\sigma\}\text{.}

Sections of
\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
are called
vertical tensor fields
of rank
(k,l)
.
15
15

15

A vertical tensor field of rank
(k,l)
on an aAdS region
\mathscr{M}
can be equivalently viewed as a
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
-parameterised family of rank
(k,l)
tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.

Definition 2.3
.

We adopt the following notational conventions and natural identifications:

•

Italicized font (as in
g
) denotes tensor fields on
\mathscr{M}
.

•

Serif font (as in
\mathsf{g}
) denotes vertical tensor fields. Any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
can be uniquely identified with a tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
by demanding that the contraction of any component of
\mathsf{A}
with
\partial_{\rho}
or
d\rho
identically vanishes.

•

Fraktur font (as in
\mathfrak{g}
) denotes tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
. If
\mathfrak{A}
is a tensor field on
\mathscr{I}
then
\mathfrak{A}
will also denote the vertical tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
obtained by extending
\mathfrak{A}
as a
\rho
-independent family of tensor fields on
\mathscr{I}
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be an aAdS region, and let
\mathsf{A}
be a vertical tensor field.

•

Given any
\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]
,
\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma}
denotes the tensor field on
\mathscr{I}
obtained from restricting
\mathsf{A}
to the level set
\{\rho=\sigma\}
and then identifying
\{\rho=\sigma\}
with
\mathscr{I}
.

•

The
\rho
-Lie derivative of
\mathsf{A}
, denoted
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}
, is defined to be the vertical tensor field satisfying

(2.3)

\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma}=\lim_{\sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\sigma}(\sigma^{\prime}-\sigma)^{-1}(\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma^{\prime}}-\mathsf{A}|_{\sigma})\text{,}\qquad\sigma\in(0,\rho_{0}]\text{.}

Next, we establish coordinate system conventions on
\mathscr{I}
and
\mathscr{M}
:

Definition 2.5
.

Suppose
\mathscr{M}
is an aAdS region and
(U,\varphi)
is a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
. We write
\varphi_{\rho}:=(\rho,\varphi)
to denote the corresponding lifted coordinates on
(0,\rho_{0}]\times U
and adopt the following notational conventions:

•

Latin indices
a,b,c,\dots
denote
\varphi
-coordinate components.

•

Greek indices
\alpha,\beta,\mu,\nu,\dots
denote
\varphi_{\rho}
-coordinate components.

Definition 2.6
.

Suppose
\mathscr{M}
is an aAdS region. A coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
is called
compact
iff
\bar{U}
is a compact subset of
\mathscr{I}
and
\varphi
extends smoothly to an open neighborhood of
\bar{U}
.

We now define a notion of magnitude for vertical tensor fields with respect to a given coordinate system and use this to make sense of boundary limits in a natural way:

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be an aAdS region and fix some
M\geq 0
.
Furthermore, let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathfrak{A}
be a rank
(k,l)
vertical tensor field and a rank
(k,l)
tensor field on
\mathscr{I}
respectively.

•

Given a compact coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
, we define
16
16

16

Each
\partial_{a_{i}}
denotes a
\varphi
–coordinate derivative.

(2.4)

|\mathsf{A}|_{M,\varphi}:=\sum_{m=0}^{M}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\\
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\\
c_{1},\dots,c_{l}\end{subarray}}|\partial^{m}_{a_{1}\dots a_{m}}\mathsf{A}^{b_{1}\dots b_{k}}_{c_{1}\dots c_{l}}|\text{.}

•

We write
\mathsf{A}\rightarrow^{M}\mathfrak{A}
iff given any compact coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
,

(2.5)

\lim_{\sigma\searrow 0}\sup_{\{\sigma\}\times U}|\mathsf{A}-\mathfrak{A}|_{M,\varphi}=0\text{.}

•

\mathsf{A}
is
weakly locally bounded
iff for any compact coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
,

(2.6)

\sup_{U}\int_{0}^{\rho_{0}}\left.\left|\mathsf{A}\right|_{0\text{,}\varphi}\right|_{\sigma}d\sigma<\infty\text{.}

•

We additionally define a local uniform norm of
\mathsf{A}
:

(2.7)

\left\lVert\mathsf{A}\right\rVert_{M\text{,}\varphi}:=\sup_{(0,\rho_{0}]\times U}\left|\mathsf{A}\right|_{M\text{,}\varphi}\text{.}

•

\mathsf{A}
is
locally bounded in
C^{M}
iff for any compact coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
,

(2.8)

\left\lVert\mathsf{A}\right\rVert_{M\text{,}\varphi}<\infty\text{.}

Now let’s use the above-defined notion of a boundary limit to rigorously define the class of metrics we’re interested in.

Definition 2.8
.

(\mathscr{M},g)
is called a
FG-aAdS segment
iff the following hold:

•

\mathscr{M}
is an aAdS region and
g
is a Lorentzian metric on
\mathscr{M}
.

•

There exists a rank
(0,2)
vertical tensor field
\mathsf{g}
such that

(2.9)

g:=\rho^{-2}(d\rho^{2}+\mathsf{g})\text{.}

•

There exists a Lorentzian metric
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathscr{I}
such that

(2.10)

\mathsf{g}\rightarrow^{0}\mathfrak{g}\text{.}

Given such a FG-aAdS segment,

•

We refer to the form (
2.9
) for
g
as the
Fefferman–Graham gauge condition
.

•

(\mathscr{I},\mathfrak{g})
is the
conformal boundary
associated with
(\mathscr{M},g,\rho)
.
17
17

17

As noted in Section
1
, there exist coordinate transformations preserving (
2.9
) but altering
\mathsf{g}
. Such transformations induce conformal transformations of
\mathfrak{g}
, so it only makes sense to speak of the conformal boundary metric up to a conformal factor.

We also define a suitable regularity class for the vertical metrics considered in this article.

Definition 2.9
.

We say that a FG-aAdS segment
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is
k
–regular
if
\mathsf{g}
is locally bounded in
C^{k+2}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}
is weakly locally bounded.

For the sake of clarity, let’s define some further notational conventions before continuing.

Definition 2.10
.

Given a FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
,

•

g^{-1}
,
\nabla
,
\nabla^{\#}
,
R
,
Rc
and
Rs
respectively denote the metric dual, Levi-Civita connection, gradient, Riemann curvature, Ricci curvature and scalar curvature with respect to
g
.

•

\mathsf{g}^{-1}
,
\mathsf{D}
,
\mathsf{D}^{\#}
,
\mathsf{R}
,
\mathsf{Rc}
and
\mathsf{Rs}
respectively denote the above objects with respect to
\mathsf{g}
.

•

\mathfrak{g}^{-1}
,
\mathfrak{D}
,
\mathfrak{D}^{\#}
,
\mathfrak{R}
,
\mathfrak{Rc}
and
\mathfrak{Rs}
respectively denote the above objects with respect to
\mathfrak{g}
.

3.
The Mixed Tensor Calculus

3.1.
The Formalism

In this section (which again follows the presentation of
[
MS20
]
), our aim is to make sense of a
g
-wave operator acting on a vertical tensor field—in such a way that it is compatible with standard covariant operations. Our first step in this direction is to construct connections on the vertical bundles which permit covariant derivatives of vertical tensor fields in all directions along
\mathscr{M}
.

Definition 3.1
.

We denote multi-indices by
\bar{\mu}:=\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{k}
. Additionally, we write

•

\hat{\mu}_{i}\![\alpha]
to denote
\bar{\mu}
with the
i^{\text{th}}
component replaced with an
\alpha
-component.

•

\hat{\mu}_{i,j}\![\alpha,\beta]
to denote
\bar{\mu}
with the
i\textsuperscript{th}
and
j\textsuperscript{th}
components replaced with
\alpha
and
\beta
components respectively.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment. There exists a unique connection
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
on
\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
such that the following hold for rank
\left(k,l\right)
vertical tensor fields
\mathsf{A}
with respect to any coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{c}\mathsf{A}^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{c}\mathsf{A}^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{b}}\text{,}

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{b}}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}^{\bar{a}}_{\bar{b}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathsf{g}^{a_{i}c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{cd}\mathsf{A}^{\hat{a}_{i}\![d]}_{\bar{b}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{b_{j}c}\mathsf{A}^{\bar{a}}_{\hat{b}_{j}\![d]}\text{.}

Furthermore, for any vector field
X
on
\mathscr{M}
,

•

The following holds for vertical tensor fields
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
:

(3.3)

\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}(\mathsf{A}\otimes\mathsf{B})=\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}\mathsf{A}\otimes\mathsf{B}+\mathsf{A}\otimes\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}\mathsf{B}\text{.}

•

The following holds for vertical tensor fields
\mathsf{A}
and tensor contraction operations
\mathcal{C}
:

(3.4)

\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}(\mathcal{C}\mathsf{A})=\mathcal{C}(\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}\mathsf{A})\text{.}

•

The connection
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
is
\mathsf{g}
–compatible:

(3.5)

\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}\mathsf{g}=0\text{,}\qquad\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{X}\mathsf{g}^{-1}=0\text{.}

Proof.

See
[
MS20
, Proposition 2.23]
.
∎

The connections
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
extend the vertical Levi-Civita connections
\mathsf{D}
to permit covariant derivatives of vertical fields in the
\rho
–direction. In order to construct the
g
-wave operator for vertical tensor fields in this spirit, we must first define some further tensorial objects on
\mathscr{M}
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment. The
mixed bundle
of rank
(\kappa,\lambda;k,l)
over
\mathscr{M}
is given by

(3.6)

T^{\kappa}_{\lambda}\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}:=T^{\kappa}_{\lambda}\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}\text{.}

Sections of
T^{\kappa}_{\lambda}\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
are called
mixed tensor fields
of rank
(\kappa,\lambda;k,l)
. Furthermore, the
bundle connection

\bar{\nabla}
on the mixed bundle
T^{\kappa}_{\lambda}\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
is defined as the tensor product connection of
\nabla
on
T^{\kappa}_{\lambda}\mathscr{M}
and
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
on
\mathsf{V}^{k}_{l}\mathscr{M}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment.
Then:

•

For any vector field
X
on
\mathscr{M}
and mixed tensor fields
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
,

(3.7)

\bar{\nabla}_{X}(\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B})=\bar{\nabla}_{X}\mathbf{A}\otimes\mathbf{B}+\mathbf{A}\otimes\bar{\nabla}_{X}\mathbf{B}\text{.}

•

For any vector field
X
on
\mathscr{M}
,

(3.8)

\bar{\nabla}_{X}g=0\text{,}\qquad\bar{\nabla}_{X}g^{-1}=0\text{,}\qquad\bar{\nabla}_{X}\mathsf{g}=0\text{,}\qquad\bar{\nabla}_{X}\mathsf{g}^{-1}=0\text{.}

Proof.

See
[
MS20
, Proposition 2.28]
.
∎

Generally speaking, the mixed connections
\bar{\nabla}
behave like
\nabla
on spacetime components and
\bar{\mathsf{D}}
on vertical components. The properties demonstrated in Proposition
3.4
are analogous to the properties of covariant derivatives that enable the standard integration by parts formulae; we are thus able to extend these directly to mixed tensor fields.

We now define higher covariant derivatives in the context of mixed bundles:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment and
\mathbf{A}
be a mixed tensor field of rank
(\kappa,\lambda;k,l)
.

•

The
mixed covariant differential
of
\mathbf{A}
is the mixed tensor field
\bar{\nabla}\mathbf{A}
, of rank
(\kappa,\lambda+1;k,l)
, that maps each vector field
X
on
\mathscr{M}
to
\bar{\nabla}_{X}\mathbf{A}
.

•

The
mixed Hessian

\bar{\nabla}^{2}\mathbf{A}
is defined as the mixed covariant differential of
\bar{\nabla}\mathbf{A}
.

•

The
wave operator

\bar{\Box}\mathbf{A}
is the
g
-trace of
\bar{\nabla}^{2}\mathbf{A}
.

3.2.
Conversion Formulae

In this article we will need to convert equations for spacetime quantities to corresponding equations for vertical quantities. In this subsection, we present a systematic method for doing so. The schematic notation and the computations involved in the proofs for this section have been assembled by Arick Shao in preparation for the upcoming work
[
HSon
]
, and kindly shared with the author for the present article.

Let’s begin by fixing schematic notations for asymptotic error terms:

Definition 3.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment, fix an integer
M\geq 0
and let
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathscr{M})
. Then
\mathscr{O}_{M}(h)
refers to any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{a}
satisfying

(3.9)

|\mathsf{a}|_{M,\varphi}\lesssim_{\varphi}h\text{,}

for any compact coordinate system
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
.

Furthermore, given a vertical tensor field
\mathsf{B}
,
\mathscr{O}_{M}(h;\mathsf{B})
refers to any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
that is expressible in the form

(3.10)

\mathsf{A}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathscr{C}_{k}(\mathsf{a}_{k}\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\ast}_{k})\text{,}

where
N\geq 0
and for each
1\leq k\leq N
,

•

\mathsf{B}^{\ast}_{k}
is
\mathsf{B}
composed with some permutation of its components.

•

\mathsf{a}_{k}
is a vertical tensor field satisfying
\mathsf{a}_{k}=\mathscr{O}_{M}(h)
.

•

\mathscr{C}_{k}
is a composition of zero or more contractions and
\mathsf{g}
-metric contraction operations.

Next, we establish some commutation identities for vertical tensor fields:

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment, fix
M\geq 2
and assume

(3.11)

\mathsf{g}=\mathscr{O}_{M}(1)\text{,}\qquad\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}=\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho)\text{.}

Then the following commutation identities hold for any vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}
:

(3.12)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}(\mathsf{D}\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}(\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\mathsf{A})+\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho;\mathsf{D}\mathsf{A})+\mathscr{O}_{M-3}(\rho;\mathsf{A})

(3.13)

\displaystyle\bar{\Box}(\rho^{p}\mathsf{A})

\displaystyle=\rho^{p}\bar{\Box}\mathsf{A}+2p\rho^{p+1}\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\mathsf{A}-p(n-p)\rho^{p}\mathsf{A}+\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{2};\rho^{p}\mathsf{A})\text{.}

Proof.

See Appendix
A.1
.
∎

We now fix some further notation so as to be able to express the conversion formulae in a compact form:

Definition 3.8
.

Suppose
A
is a tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
of rank
(0,r_{1}+r_{2})
, where
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
, and let
\mathsf{A}
be the corresponding rank
(0,r_{2})
vertical tensor field defined with respect to any coordinates
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
by

(3.14)

\mathsf{A}_{\bar{a}}:=A_{\bar{\rho}\bar{a}}\text{,}

where the multi-index
\bar{\rho}:=\rho\dots\rho
represents
r_{1}
copies of
\rho
, while
\bar{a}:=a_{1}\dots a_{r_{2}}
. Then:

•

For any
1\leq i\leq r_{1}
, the rank
(0,r_{2}+1)
vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i}
is given by

(3.15)

(\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{b\bar{a}}:=A_{\hat{\rho}_{i}\![b]\bar{a}}\text{,}

•

For any
1\leq j\leq r_{2}
, the rank
(0,r_{2}-1)
vertical tensor field
\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j}
is given by

(3.16)

(\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\hat{a}_{j}}:=A_{\bar{\rho}\hat{a}_{j}\![\rho]}\text{,}

•

For any
1\leq i,j\leq r_{1}
with
i\neq j
, the rank
(0,r_{2}+2)
vertical field
\mathsf{A}^{\rho,\rho}_{i,j}
is given by

(3.17)

(\mathsf{A}^{\rho,\rho}_{i,j})_{cb\bar{a}}:=A_{\hat{\rho}_{i,j}\![c,b]\bar{a}}\text{.}

•

For any
1\leq i,j\leq r_{2}
with
i\neq j
, the rank
(0,r_{2}-2)
vertical field
\mathsf{A}^{v,v}_{i,j}
is given by

(3.18)

(\mathsf{A}^{v,v}_{i,j})_{\hat{a}_{i,j}}:=A_{\bar{\rho}\hat{a}_{i,j}\![\rho,\rho]}\text{.}

•

For any
1\leq i\leq r_{1}
and
1\leq j\leq r_{2}
, the rank
(0,r_{2})
vertical field
\mathsf{A}^{\rho,v}_{i,j}
is given by

(3.19)

(\mathsf{A}^{\rho,v}_{i,j})_{b\hat{a}_{j}}:=A_{\hat{\rho}_{i}\![b]\hat{a}_{j}\![\rho]}\text{.}

We are now in a position to state the conversion formulae:

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(\mathscr{M},g)
be a FG-aAdS segment and assume (
3.11
) holds for some
M\geq 2
.
Let
A
be a tensor field on
\mathscr{M}
of rank
(0,r_{1}+r_{2})
, where
r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
, and let
\mathsf{A}
be the associated rank
(0,r_{2})
vertical tensor field defined by (
3.14
).

Then the following identities hold with respect to any coordinates
(U,\varphi)
on
\mathscr{I}
:

(3.20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\rho}A_{\bar{\rho}\bar{a}}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-r_{1}-r_{2}}\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}(\rho^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\mathsf{A})_{\bar{a}}\text{,}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\nabla_{c}A_{\bar{\rho}\bar{a}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{c}\mathsf{A}_{\bar{a}}+(\rho^{-1}\delta^{b}_{c}-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{bd}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{dc})\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}(\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{b\bar{a}}-\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}(\rho^{-1}\mathsf{g}_{ca_{j}}-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ca_{j}})\,(\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\hat{a}_{j}}\text{.}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{c}\mathsf{A}_{\bar{a}}+\rho^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}(\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{c\bar{a}}-\rho^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\mathsf{g}_{ca_{j}}\,(\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\hat{a}_{j}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho;\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{c\bar{a}}+\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho;\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{c\bar{a}}\text{,}

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Box A_{\bar{\rho}\bar{a}}

\displaystyle=\rho^{-r_{1}-r_{2}}\bar{\Box}(\rho^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\mathsf{A})_{\bar{a}}+2\rho\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathsf{g}^{bc}\,\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{b}(\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{c\bar{a}}-2\rho\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{a_{j}}(\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\hat{a}_{j}}-(nr_{1}+r_{2})\,\mathsf{A}_{\bar{a}}

\displaystyle\qquad-2\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}(\mathsf{A}^{\rho,v}_{i,j})_{a_{j}\hat{a}_{j}}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r_{1}}\mathsf{g}^{bc}\,(\mathsf{A}^{\rho,\rho}_{i,j})_{bc\bar{a}}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r_{2}}\mathsf{g}_{a_{i}a_{j}}\,(\mathsf{A}^{v,v}_{i,j})_{\hat{a}_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{\bar{a}}+\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\bar{a}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\mathscr{O}_{M-3}(\rho^{3};\mathsf{A}^{\rho}_{i})_{\bar{a}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\mathscr{O}_{M-3}(\rho^{3};\mathsf{A}^{v}_{j})_{\bar{a}}+\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{A})_{\bar{a}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{r_{2}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{A}^{\rho,v}_{i,j})_{\bar{a}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r_{1}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{A}^{\rho,\rho}_{i,j})_{\bar{a}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r_{2}}\mathscr{O}_{M-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{A}^{v,v}_{i,j})_{\bar{a}}\text{,}

Proof.

See Appendix
A.2
.
∎

4.
Vacuum Spacetimes

4.1.
The Metric

Definition 4.1
.

An
(n+1)
–dimensional FG-aAdS segment
(\mathscr{M},g)
is called a
vacuum FG-aAdS segment
iff it satisfies (
1.1
).

The following boundary limits were derived in
[
Sha20
, Theorem 3.3]
:

Theorem 4.2
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose that
(\mathscr{M},g)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Then

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}\rightarrow^{M_{0}}\,\mathfrak{g}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{g}^{-1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}}\,\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\text{,}

and, for
0\leq k<n
, there exists tensor fields
\mathfrak{g}^{(k)}
on
\mathscr{I}
such that

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k}\mathsf{g}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k}\,k!\,\mathfrak{g}^{(k)}\text{,}\qquad\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k+1}\mathsf{g}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k}\,0\text{,}

where

•

\mathfrak{g}^{(0)}=\mathfrak{g}

.

•

\mathfrak{g}^{(2)}=-\mathfrak{p}
, where
\mathfrak{p}
is the
\mathfrak{g}
–Schouten tensor:

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}=\frac{1}{n-2}\left(\mathfrak{Rc}-\frac{1}{2(n-2)}\mathfrak{Rs}\cdot\mathfrak{g}\right)\text{.}

•

\mathfrak{g}^{(k)}=0
if
n
is odd.

Furthermore, there exist tensor fields
\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(\dagger)}

18
18

18

\mathfrak{g}^{\dagger}
is in
C^{M_{0}-n}
on
\mathscr{I}
.
on
\mathscr{I}
such that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n+1}\mathsf{g}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,n!\,\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}\text{,}\qquad\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n}\mathfrak{g}-n!\,(\log\rho)\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,n!\,\mathfrak{g}^{(\dagger)}\text{,}

where
\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}=0
if
n
is odd.

Note that the above result implies that any vacuum FG-aAdS segment is a ‘strongly’ FG-aAdS segment, as defined in
[
MS20
, Definition 2.13]
; this was the condition required for the Carleman estimate (and hence the unique continuation result) of that article to hold.

Using the above limits, the following precise statement of the Fefferman-Graham expansion (
1.7
) for sufficiently regular vacuum FG-aAdS segments was given in
[
Sha20
, Theorem 3.6]
:
19
19

19

Similar expansions were also derived for
\mathsf{R}
and
\mathsf{D}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}
. For the sake of brevity, we do not reproduce these here.

Corollary 4.3
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose that
(\mathscr{M},g)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Let
\mathfrak{g}^{(k)}
for
0\leq k<n
and
\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}
be as in Theorem
4.2
. Then there exists a
C^{M_{0}-n}
tensor field
\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}
on
\mathscr{I}
and a vertical tensor field
\mathsf{r}
such that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\rho^{2k}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}+\rho^{n}\,\mathsf{r}\text{,}\qquad&\text{$n$ odd}\text{,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\rho^{2k}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n}\log\rho\,\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}+\rho^{n}\,\mathfrak{g}^{(n)}+\rho^{n}\,\mathsf{r}\text{,}\qquad&\text{$n$ even}\text{,}\end{cases}

where the ‘remainder’
\mathsf{r}
satisfies

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}\to^{M_{0}-n}\,\,0\text{.}

Remark 4.4
.

The above shows that if
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment then (
3.11
) holds for
M=M_{0}
.

4.2.
The Weyl Curvature

Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose that
(\mathscr{M},g)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Let
(U,\varphi)
be a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
.
Then the following hold with respect to
\varphi_{\rho}
-coordinates:

(4.7)

Rc_{\alpha\beta}=-n\cdot g_{\alpha\beta}\text{,}\qquad Rs=-n(n+1)\text{,}\qquad W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}+g_{\alpha\gamma}g_{\beta\delta}-g_{\alpha\delta}g_{\beta\gamma}\text{,}

where
W
is the
g
--Weyl curvature.
20
20

20

That is, the
g
–traceless part of the
g
–Riemann curvature.
Furthermore, the Weyl curvature satisfies the wave equation

(4.8)

Proof.

The first two identities in (
4.7
) follow by taking the trace of (
1.1
). The third identity is a substitution of these expressions into the Weyl curvature,

(4.9)

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}=R_{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}-\frac{2}{n-1}\left(g_{\alpha[\gamma}Rc_{\delta]\beta}-g_{\beta[\gamma}Rc_{\delta]\alpha}\right)+\frac{2}{n(n-1)}Rs\cdot g_{\alpha[\gamma}g_{\delta]\beta}\text{.}

\displaystyle\nabla_{[\mu}W_{\alpha\beta]\gamma\delta}=0\text{,}

) gives

in which the second term vanishes by (
4.11
). For the remaining terms, one uses (
4.7
) to replace the Riemann curvature with the Weyl curvature and the metric. After simplifying, one finds

eta}{\lambda}{\mu}}}}-2\mathchoice{W^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}}{W^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\delta}}}}{W^{{{\lambda}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.63474pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17776pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.55554pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\make playstyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\lambda}{\beta}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\lambda}{\beta}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\lambda}{\beta}{\mu}{\gamma}}}}=0\text{,}

from which (
4.8
) follows immediately.
∎

Our goal is to use the mixed covariant formalism to convert the spacetime wave equation (
4.8
) into a system of wave equations for each of the independent vertical components of the Weyl curvature, defined below.

Definition 4.6
.

Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a FG-aAdS segment and
\left(U,\varphi\right)
is a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
. With respect to
\varphi_{\rho}
–coordinates, we define the following independent vertical components of the Weyl curvature:

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}:=\rho^{2}W_{abcd}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{1}_{abc}:=\rho^{2}W_{\rho abc}\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}:=\rho^{2}W_{\rho a\rho b}\text{.}

Remark 4.7
.

Since the spacetime Weyl curvature is trace-free, one has that

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}=g^{\rho\rho}W_{\rho a\rho b}=-g^{cd}W_{cadb}=-\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{cadb}.

In other words,
-\mathsf{W}^{2}
is a
\mathsf{g}
–trace of
\mathsf{W}^{0}
.

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a FG-aAdS segment and
\left(U,\varphi\right)
is a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
. With respect to
\varphi_{\rho}
–coordinates, the
\mathsf{g}
-traceless part of
\mathsf{W}^{0}
is

(4.18)

\hat{\mathsf{W}}^{0}_{abcd}:=\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}+\frac{2}{n-2}\left(\mathsf{g}_{a[c}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{d]b}+\mathsf{g}_{b[d}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{c]a}\right)\text{.}

In
[
Sha20
]
it was demonstrated that the vertical components of the Weyl curvature for a vacuum FG-aAdS segment can be expressed in terms of
\mathsf{g}
,
\mathsf{g}^{-1}
,
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}
and
\mathsf{R}
:

Proposition 4.9
.

[
Sha20
, Proposition 2.25]

Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a vacuum FG-aAdS segment and
\left(U,\varphi\right)
is a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
. Then the following relations hold with respect to
\varphi
–coordinates:

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{R}_{abcd}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{a[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]b}+\rho^{-1}\left(\mathsf{g}_{a[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]b}-\mathsf{g}_{b[c}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d]a}\right)\text{,}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{1}_{cab}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{D}_{[b}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{a]c}\text{,}

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{2}\mathsf{g}_{ab}+\frac{1}{2}\rho^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ab}+\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ac}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{bd}\text{.}

The limits proved in
[
Sha20
, Theorem 3.6]
may be straightforwardly applied to derive similar limits for derivatives of the vertical components of the Weyl curvature, and hence to write down Fefferman-Graham expansions similar to (
4.5
):

Theorem 4.10
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose that
(\mathscr{M},g)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Then, for
0\leq k<n-2
and
i=0,1,2
, there exist tensor fields
\mathfrak{W}_{i}^{k}
on
\mathscr{I}
such that

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k}\mathsf{W}^{0}

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,k!\,\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(k)}\text{,}\qquad\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k+1}\mathsf{W}^{0}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,0\text{,}\qquad 0\leq k<n-2\text{,}

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k}\mathsf{W}^{1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,k!\,\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(k)}\text{,}\qquad\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k+1}\mathsf{W}^{1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,0\text{,}\qquad 0\leq k<n-1\text{,}

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k}\mathsf{W}^{2}

\displaystyle\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,k!\,\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(k)}\text{,}\qquad\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{k+1}\mathsf{W}^{2}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-k-2}\,0\text{,}\qquad 0\leq k<n-2\text{,}

where
\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(k)}=\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(k)}=0
if
k
is odd and
\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(k)}=0
if
k
is even. Furthermore, there exist tensor fields
\mathfrak{W}_{i}^{(\star)}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{i}^{(\dagger)}
on
\mathscr{I}
such that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n-1}\mathsf{W}^{0}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,(n-2)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(\star)}\text{,}\\
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n-2}\mathsf{W}^{0}-(n-2)!\,(\log\rho)\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(\star)}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,(n-2)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(\dagger)}\text{,}\end{cases}

(4.26)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n}\mathsf{W}^{1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n-1}\,(n-1)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(\star)}\text{,}\\
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n-1}\mathsf{W}^{1}-(n-1)!\,(\log\rho)\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(\star)}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n-1}\,(n-1)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(\dagger)}\text{,}\end{cases}

(4.27)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n-1}\mathsf{W}^{2}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,(n-2)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(\star)}\text{,}\\
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{n-2}\mathsf{W}^{2}-(n-2)!\,(\log\rho)\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(\star)}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-n}\,(n-2)!\,\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(\dagger)}\text{,}\end{cases}

where
\mathfrak{W}_{i}^{(\star)}=0
if
n
is odd.

Corollary 4.11
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Then, with respect to any compact coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
,

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\rho^{2k}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(n-2)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{0}\text{,}&\text{$n$ odd,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-4}{2}}\rho^{2k}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n-2}\log\rho\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(\star)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(n-2)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{0}\text{,}&\text{$n$ even,}\end{cases}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\rho^{2k+1}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(2k+1)}+\rho^{n-1}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(n-1)}+\rho^{n-1}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{1}\text{,}&\text{$n$ odd,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-4}{2}}\rho^{2k+1}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(2k+1)}+\rho^{n-1}\log\rho\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(\star)}+\rho^{n-1}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(n-1)}+\rho^{n-1}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{1}\text{,}&\text{$n$ even,}\end{cases}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathsf{W}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}\rho^{2k}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(n-2)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{2}\text{,}&\text{$n$ odd,}\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\frac{n-4}{2}}\rho^{2k}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(2k)}+\rho^{n-2}\log\rho\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(\star)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(n-2)}+\rho^{n-2}\cdot\mathsf{r}_{2}\text{,}&\text{$n$ even,}\end{cases}

where:

•

\mathfrak{W}_{0}^{(n-2)}
,
\mathfrak{W}_{1}^{(n-1)}
and
\mathfrak{W}_{2}^{(n-2)}
are
C^{M_{0}-n}
,
C^{M_{0}-n-1}
and
C^{M_{0}-n}

\mathscr{I}
–tensor fields respectively.

•

\mathsf{r}_{0}
,
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
are vertical tensor fields for which

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathsf{r}_{0}\to^{M_{0}-n}0\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{r}_{1}\to^{M_{0}-n-1}0\text{,}\qquad\mathsf{r}_{2}\to^{M_{0}-n}0\text{.}

In fact, one can use Proposition
4.9
to compute the leading–order terms in each of these boundary expansions:

Proposition 4.12
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is a
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. Then

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}_{0\,abcd}^{(0)}=\mathfrak{W}_{abcd}\text{,}\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{1\,abc}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{n-2}\mathfrak{C}_{abc}\text{,}\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(0)}=0\text{,}

where
\mathfrak{W}
and
\mathfrak{C}
are the
\mathfrak{g}
–Weyl curvature and Cotton tensors respectively, and

(4.33)

\displaystyle n=3:\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(1)}=-\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{g}^{(3)}_{ab}\text{,}

(4.34)

\displaystyle n=4:\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(\star)}=-4\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}_{ab}\text{,}\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(2)}=-3\mathfrak{g}^{(\star)}_{ab}-4\mathfrak{g}^{(4)}_{ab}+\mathfrak{g}^{cd}\mathfrak{p}_{ac}\mathfrak{p}_{bd}\text{,}

(4.35)

\displaystyle n>4:\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(2)}=-4\mathfrak{g}^{(4)}_{ab}+\mathfrak{g}^{cd}\mathfrak{p}_{ac}\mathfrak{p}_{bd}\text{,}

(4.36)

\displaystyle n>6:\qquad\mathfrak{W}_{2\,ab}^{(4)}=-12\mathfrak{g}^{(6)}_{ab}-2\mathfrak{g}^{cd}\left(\mathfrak{p}_{ac}\mathfrak{g}^{(4)}_{bd}+\mathfrak{g}^{(4)}_{ac}\mathfrak{p}_{bd}\right)+\mathfrak{g}^{ce}\mathfrak{g}^{df}\mathfrak{p}_{ef}\mathfrak{p}_{ac}\mathfrak{p}_{bd}\text{.}

Proof.

Substituting the appropriate expansions into the right-hand sides of (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) yield

(4.37)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}_{0\,abcd}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{abcd}+2\mathfrak{g}_{a[c}\mathfrak{p}_{d]b}-2\mathfrak{g}_{b[c}\mathfrak{p}_{d]a}=\mathfrak{W}_{abcd}\text{,}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{W}_{1\,abc}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=2\,\mathfrak{D}_{[c}\mathfrak{p}_{b]a}=\frac{1}{n-2}\mathfrak{C}_{abc}\text{,}

as required. To prove the remaining expressions, we first require the leading-order terms in the boundary expansion of
\mathsf{g}^{-1}
. These are obtained by taking derivatives of

(4.38)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}^{ab}\mathsf{g}_{bc}=\delta^{a}_{c}\text{,}

and computing boundary limits by applying Theorem
4.2
. In particular, one finds that
21
21

21

Additional limits are computed for the case when
\mathfrak{g}
is conformally flat in Corollary
6.4
.

(4.39)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}}\,\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\text{,}\qquad\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-1}\,0\text{,}\qquad\mathscr{L}_{\rho}^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\rightarrow^{M_{0}-2}\,2\,\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\mathfrak{p}\text{.}

Substituting this along with the boundary expansions of
\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}
and

(on a dimensional case-by-case basis) into the right-hand side of (
4.21
) yields the required expressions.
∎

The second Bianchi identity for the spacetime Weyl curvature can be decomposed to obtain a series of first-order equations involving only vertical objects:

Proposition 4.13
.

Fix
n>2
and
M_{0}\geq n+2
. Suppose
\left(\mathscr{M},g\right)
is an
(n+1)
–dimensional,
M_{0}
–regular vacuum FG-aAdS segment. If
\left(U,\varphi\right)
is a coordinate system on
\mathscr{I}
then the following
vertical Bianchi equations
hold for the vertical Weyl fields with respect to
\varphi_{\rho}
–coordinates:

(4.40)

\displaystyle\rho\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{abcd}

\displaystyle=2\rho\mathsf{D}_{[a}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{b]cd}-2\mathsf{g}_{c[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]d}+2\mathsf{g}_{d[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]c}

\displaystyle\qquad\underbrace{+\rho\mathsf{g}^{ef}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{e[a}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{b]fcd}+\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{c[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]d}-\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]c}}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{W}^{0})}\text{,}

(4.41)

\displaystyle\rho^{n-2}\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\left(\rho^{-(n-2)}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{abc}\right)

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{g}^{de}\mathsf{D}_{d}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{eabc}-\underbrace{\mathsf{g}^{de}\left(\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{e[b}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{c]da}+\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d[e}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{a]bc}\right)}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{1})}\text{,}

(4.42)

\displaystyle\rho\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\left(\rho^{-1}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{abc}\right)

\displaystyle=2\mathsf{D}_{[b}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{c]a}\underbrace{-\mathsf{g}^{de}\left(\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{be}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{(a|d|c)}-\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ce}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{(a|d|b)}\right)}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{1})}\text{,}

(4.43)

\displaystyle\rho^{n-2}\bar{\mathsf{D}}_{\rho}\left(\rho^{-(n-2)}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{bd}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\mathsf{g}^{ac}\mathsf{D}_{[a}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{b]cd}

\displaystyle\qquad-\underbrace{\mathsf{g}^{ac}\left(\mathsf{g}^{ef}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{e[a}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{b]fcd}+\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{c[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]d}-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{da}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{bc}\right)}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{0})}\text{.}

.

\displaystyle\qquad\underbrace{-\rho\,\mathsf{g}^{ef}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{e[c}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{d]fab}+\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{c[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]d}-\rho\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{d[a}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{b]c}}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{W}^{0})}\text{,}

\displaystyle\rho^{n-2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\left(\rho^{-(n-2)}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{abc}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\underbrace{\qquad\qquad-\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{bd}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{eac}-\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{cd}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{cba}\big)}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{1})}\text{,}

\displaystyle=2\mathsf{D}_{[b}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{c]a}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{de}\left(\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{be}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{cda}-\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ce}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{bda}+\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ae}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{dcb}\right)}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{1})}\text{,}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\rho^{n-2}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\left(\rho^{-(n-2)}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{bd}\right)

\displaystyle=-2\mathsf{g}^{ac}\mathsf{D}_{[a}\mathsf{W}^{1}_{b]cd}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{g}^{ac}\mathsf{g}^{ef}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{ea}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{bfcd}+\mathsf{g}^{ef}\mathscr{L}_{\rho}\mathsf{g}_{e[b}\mathsf{W}^{2}_{f]d}}_{\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{0})}\text{.}

Proof.

Consider the following components of the spacetime Bianchi equation (

):

\displaystyle=0\text{,}

(4.49)

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}W_{\rho bcd}+\nabla_{\rho}W_{bacd}+\nabla_{b}W_{a\rho cd}

\displaystyle=0\text{,}

(4.50)

\displaystyle\nabla_{d}W_{eabc}+\nabla_{b}W_{eacd}+\nabla_{c}W_{eadb}

\displaystyle=0\text{.}

•

) follows by application of (

Similarly, the wave equation satisfied by the spacetime Weyl curvature can be decomposed into a series of wave equations involving only vertical objects:

Proposition 4.15
.

Fix

and

(4.51)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{W}^{1})\text{,}

(4.52)

\displaystyle\left(\bar{\Box}_{g}+(n-1)\right)\mathsf{W}^{1}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-3}(\rho^{3};\hat{\mathsf{W}}^{0})+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{W}^{1})+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-3}(\rho^{3};\mathsf{W}^{2})\vskip 5.69054pt

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\hat{\mathsf{W}}^{0})+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{W}^{2})\text{,}

(4.53)

\displaystyle\left(\bar{\Box}_{g}+2(n-2)\right)\mathsf{W}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{2};\hat{\mathsf{W}}^{0})+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-3}(\rho^{3};\mathsf{W}^{1})+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{2};\mathsf{W}^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathscr{O}_{M_{0}-2}(\rho^{3};\bar{\mathsf{D}}\mathsf{W}^{1})\text{.}

(

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}_{M_{0}-2}\left(1;\hat{\mathsf{W}}^{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{M_{0}-3}\left(\rho;\mathsf{W}^{1}\right)+\mathcal{O}_{M_{0}-2}\left(1;\mathsf{W}^{2}\right)\text{.}

\displaystyle\qquad-2(n-1)\mathsf{W}^{2}_{ab}+2\mathsf{g}^{cd}\mathsf{W}^{0}_{cadb}
